ParlaMint-GR_2019-08-05-S1-commons.u6	Ladies and gentlemen, the first emblematic bill tabled by our Government is a great reform of the functioning of the government and state. Breakdown, because it places itself on a new, modern and innovative basis the functioning of the government and state, which must meet the needs and requirements of modern times. Dispersible is the concept in Greek society that we have an incompetent state. Which one of us, in contact with the citizens, has not heard the phrase: “What are you waiting for? There is no State.’ This perception is largely well founded and is based on the shortcomings, inability, inefficiency, inefficiency, mistrust with which the state is often dealing with citizens. 'Build a state', then. This is the mandate we have from the Greek society. It is the mandate that this government has and that is what it is doing today. It sets the foundation for this state to be built. But what state do we really want? We want a state of modern, well-organised and effective, that meets the needs of citizens and society. We want a state of citizens-friendlyness, which will effectively and in a short time, without having to search for cross-cutting instruments for this, which will facilitate entrepreneurship and operate as a catalyst in the economy, as an accelerator and not as an accelerator in development, which will respond and operate directly, coordinatedly and effectively in exceptional circumstances and in crisis situations. The example of the confusion, the inadequacy and inefficiency of the state machine a year ago in the Eye is typical. So what are the characteristics of a modern and well-organised state? First of all, the modern state operates with principles and rules, where everyone, each string on the state machine, from the first and the highest positioned to the last, will know its role, its work and the rules and principles on which it operates. This is how harmony and order are achieved in administration. A key factor for the state to function properly is man, human element. Trust, merit, growth based on merit and efficiency, awareness and satisfaction that your work is recognised, are the principles and values that must govern the functioning of the public administration. These, together with the continuous improvement of skills, education in new technologies, particularly information technology, in order to make the most of the possibilities offered by technologies and rapid developments to them and the continuing retraining on the development of the subject of each official's competence, are the guarantees for the harmonious and effective functioning of a modern state. So all this takes account of the bill we are discussing today. The draft law in Article 19 describes in detail the principles governing the functioning of the State and central administration. It is the principle of the rule of law, as specialises in the principles of legality and good administration, transparency and accountability, efficiency and efficiency, necessity and subsidiarity, merit and professionalism. The principles of confidence, dignity, neutrality and depopulation of the state and administration shall be strengthened by the arrangements of the draft law and shall apply to it as a whole. Here, then, are some of these arrangements: The establishment of the Office's position with a three-year term, renewable three years, from the public service hierarchy, with essential powers for the administrative and financial operation of the Ministry, coordination of the services, the power to sign a final signature, which is not chosen by the Government, but through ASA and clear and transparent, crystallised procedures. The delegation of responsibilities and in particular the final signature of all individual administrative acts to the Directors-General again taking prestige. The replacement of the head of audit bodies, such as the ILO, the IEA, the IEA and the Environment Inspectors, who have so far been posted General or Special Secretaries, i.e. political persons selected by the relevant government, who are now replaced by the Directors-General, i.e. high-level civil servants on the basis of objective criteria through the Services Councils. The annual meeting of the Prime Minister and the Ministerial Council with the Directors-General of the Ministries in January each year. What do they look like? All of this shows the confidence of the New Democracy Government, the confidence of the Mitsotakis Government in the worthy public officials who trust them and give them substantial powers. This is how another myth of today's Antipopulation and yesterday's government is being destroyed, namely that Mitsotakis does not trust civil servants, that Mitsotakis will be after civil servants, that he will destroy them more many dangers, which we have heard mainly during the election period. On the contrary, this government is coming and trusts in practice the civil servant who wants him to be a real partner, which is why he is given substantial powers. So, trust in civil servants, but also merits these features, are these values, these principles governing the draft law, and these, together with the provision of quality, upgraded human resources. It's very typical of the importance that the bill gives to them. On this occasion I would like to congratulate the Minister, who, during the debate on the bill in the committee responsible, showed an unprecedented sensitivity to parliamentary data, taking into account all the comments, wherever they came from, either from the institutions, or from the Members of Parliament of the House of Commons and Opposition, in order to improve the draft law, which he has done to the greatest extent. So the graduates of the National School of Public Administration have the treatment that they really deserve, because the Greek state has wasted, given a lot of money to build this school and to have the students of this school and get out of there executives, who can deserve to manoeuvre the government. There is also the establishment of new sectors, such as legislators, public policy analysts, digital policy analysts. So ensuring quality human resources is one of the predictions of this bill. This, together with the exploitation of technologies and all modern means and the appropriate refurbishment equipment, can actually give a new impetus and change the way the central state operates. But modern, organised and efficient state also means planning, namely forecasting, planning and targeting. It also means implementation of the actions and actions planned within a given time, i.e. timing and monitoring of policy implementation and finally measuring the result, i.e. performance. That's what we're interested in. I think we all agree that the best return is guaranteed through programming. The benefits are maximized. Costs and total costs are minimised. The results shall be accelerated. Thus, the state becomes more efficient, more effective. It can cope with and satisfy the needs of the citizens - each one separately - but also the social system as a whole, quickly and successfully. The government itself, on the basis of these principles, works: targeting, monitoring the implementation of objectives and measuring the outcome. All this is the aim of the bill we are discussing today. In particular, in relation to planning and objectives, a strong support centre is set up at central level next to the Prime Minister, in Article 21 of the bill, the presidency of the government. It is a strong support centre for the Prime Minister's work that coordinates the planning and monitoring of the implementation of the government programme. The same happens, however, in all areas: economy, education, health, welfare, social policy, security. The Ministries finally have a final role and operate on the basis of a concrete action plan that is measurable. At the same time, there is central planning and monitoring of achieving the objectives. The Ministerial Council acquires a central, substantial, effective policy-making role and meets, not formally, to meet communication needs, but essentially, on a regular basis every month and exceptionally whenever necessary. In December each year, the Ministerial Council is introduced and approved the Consolidated Government Policy Plan and the Ministries' Action Plans. These are provided for in Article 49 et seq. of the bill. A specific role is also the ministerial councils of April and September, which are the planning and evaluation boards of the government project. The bill even introduces incompatibility and impediments to the ministers, the Secretary-General and the executives of the agencies, i.e. to all senior political executives, so that there is no conflict of interest and sets clear, clear rules in the functioning of these institutions in relation to qualities, both earlier and during the term of office, but even for a period after leaving office. It introduces rules of good legislation and codification of existing legislation. We all know, especially those who deal with the application of law, the great problem of the wrongdoing and of the pluralism that tortures the lawmakers. Here, then, the government comes to adopt, firstly, the rules of good legislation and, secondly, the existing laws, the possibility of codifying legislation, so that there are no doubts about the existing provisions and that any conflicts - if any - will be eliminated. In the fourth part of the draft law, the National Transparency Authority is institutionalised which incorporates all the scattered public and private corruption control structures that are currently in place, such as the Public Administration Inspectors' Body, Public Works Inspectorate, Health, etc. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member hits the warning bell.]] Mr President, I will need two or three minutes to finish. I would therefore like to point out that this National Transparency Authority, which is described in detail both its powers and its operating methods, presents annually a report on the evaluation and programming of Parliament's Committee on Institutions and Transparency on 31 March, and that is very important, because this principle - let me not use the term 'legalised' - but informs the relevant committee of Parliament and through this National Representation of its programming and its accounts. This is also very important. So this law is the foundation for the new operation of the state. We naturally start from the central state at the level of the government and the ministries. What we are seeking, ladies and gentlemen, is, as I said at the outset, a more efficient state for the benefit of the citizen and society. What we are seeking is the recovery of the citizens' confidence in the state, of this lost confidence and the creation of a new relationship between the state and the citizen, and through the restoration of this confidence between the state and the citizen, a new relationship between the citizen and the political system. That's the bet, that's what it is. It makes sense that there is a large part of the civilian staff who are adapted to old attitudes and perceptions, which is stuck with the old one, with established attitudes, with established practices and perceptions of the past, who wants the client state to exist just to have reason to exist. It wants to be partisan in order to serve other interests and other reasonable ones. It makes sense, after so many years of implementing such policies, that there are reactions. Not everyone can follow, we understand that, but that does not mean that these attitudes and these established practices will become an obstacle to moving forward. Because the Greek society that has suffered so many over the last decade with this great monumental cycle and the pressure it has suffered from it, a memorandum that is largely due to this poor and customer function of the state, is now mature to accept these changes, not only is mature, but encourages us to make these changes. Wherever we have been, all my fellow Members in our provinces, in society, in the world, have been in contact with the citizens, who have all told us with one voice: "Continue as you have started, do not stop. Continue with the same momentum!' This is the command: "Do change, change the state". And yes, well, we're moving on, we're not stopping. At the same moment we continue with the aim of changing the state, changing the economy, changing everything, because that is the mandate of the Greek people! Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-08-05-S1-commons.u10	Mr President, thank you for the welcome. Good to see you! Ladies and gentlemen, in the post-war political era there is no government - or even a party - that does not accompany its programme with an idea of administrative reform. Each government estimates that its own proposal for administrative reform is the one that was missing until the time she was arrested. And each of the Ministers, even when it comes to administrative microdispositions, takes its ideas as a pioneering step in the development of the administrative system. And we usually come back to a younger age, to the conclusion that nothing essential has changed. The government, which is currently proposing the bill for the alleged state of law, is subject to the same mistake and I must remind the honourable professor, on my right hand, that the administrative changes which are really important are those which society demands on the demand for administrative services and are guaranteed at the end and in its conscience. Let us say, if you ask the citizen what he considers to be a serious administrative reform, he would mention the incision made by Law 2190, the Peponnes Act, as the most characteristic attack on customer relations. In his conscience, the attempt of an organised system of transparency, as reflected in the law on "GENERAL", which, in fact, many on the right and on the left attempted to chant, but failed because of political and social response, has been recorded. A third important reform is the successive waves of strengthening the degrees of self-government, the establishment of two levels of selfgovernment and their strengthening with responsibilities, although they have not, to date, been accompanied by appropriate financial resources in their exercise. The Government therefore submits, as it claims, a draft law on the final state. Now, ladies and gentlemen, we must consult on the scientific and political conception of the term. When we are talking about a state, we are talking about the state - a headquarters that is planning and planning the general policies of the state. From this final state, which is articulated in a few ministries, with a few addresses and staff of very high scientific qualifications, the executive powers at both levels and decentralised services are transferred, almost all, to the two degrees of self-government. Only then can there be an ending state. The final state is not, in other words, top arrangements, but a profound structural reform that gives the central state the characteristics of a headquarters it plans, but its executive powers, other than justice, foreign policy and taxation, are transferred to the decentralised administrative structures at both levels of local government. And if you think that we can disagree on this concept, we have nothing but to look at your peers or major journalists from the beginning of the 20th century to the present day. Is this the case with the government's draft law, such a structural reform for the final state? Oh, no. They're top arrangements. Throughout the long pre-election period, the government carried out a propaganda picture, which was reproduced by willing journalists, that it would be a new system of prime ministerial power, relatives of the organization of the German Chancellor or the Cabinet Office of the United Kingdom. In fact, none of this is happening. There are the simulations, but with an excellent, huge difference. Government regulations do not touch what the Germans and the British have achieved in their public administration, namely strong and with great powers decentralized structures. The Länder in Germany have excellent economic and administrative powers - which is certainly not the case here - while the British, apart from the decentralised structures, have organised a state extremely efficient. It's not here. So there are administrative micro-administrations and a great deal of intervention - it is the essence of the draft legislation under discussion - which is the formation of an autonomous public service, the presidency of the Government, through which the prime minister system is strengthened. The pre-centralised system has been constantly strengthened in our country since the 1985 constitutional reform and then with many political malfunctions. As many times as political aberrations have been sought from the over-concentration of power in the Prime Minister, they have not been found to deal with political and social tensions. Today we are taking another step towards strengthening the pre-accession system. This independent public service, which comes under the Prime Minister, ladies and gentlemen, has five General Secretariats, one Special Secretariat, that is to say, a total of six Secretariats, has one Directorate-General - I will comment after the Directorate-General - the Directorate-General of the Office. In other words, the coordination and supervision of the Prime Minister is without a sign of dangerous missions, such as the management of the National Printing Office, which is now seconded by the Ministry of Administrative Reconstruction. A great deal of work and coordination, I have to say. The presidency of the Government has at least 100 post-call officials, with three hundred seconded, a total of four hundred forty, whose choice begins and ends with the wish of the Prime Minister. There is, in other words, no guarantee of a impartial crisis, such as the choices of staff or the movements of personnel within the central administration. It is a decisive responsibility for the Prime Minister. He chooses the posters, he chooses the seconded persons by way of derogation from any other provision. To them, of course, you will add staff to the diplomatic services, to the Security Bodies and to the Armed Forces. In addition to the four hundred forty people who make up the presidency of the government, it is certainly moving - it could not be otherwise - all the staff of the National Printing Office, which has so far been a special secretariat with a posted head, and therefore increased qualifications. Now he's gonna be a general manager. The attempt to over-concentrate powers around the Prime Minister also gives rise to the Scientific Service of the Parliament. I was listening, during the discussions in the parliamentary committee, to the Minister of State's statement 'no responsibility for the Ministers is at all affected. No public administration activity shall be affected.’ I pointed out to him at that time - and I would point it out today in plenary - that the Secretary-General for Legal and Parliamentary Affairs is one of the five Secretary-Generals of the Government Presidency and the final author of the bills and amendments. ParlaMint-GR_2019-08-05-S1-commons.u15	Thank you, Mr President. With this bill, the government is attempting to show these changes as neutral and naive, as necessary for the good - supposedly - of the whole society. The truth is, however, that these are the suggestions of the European Union, the Commission and the OECD, in order to make a manual for achieving the objectives of capital on the basis of what has already been legislated at the expense of the people with the three Memorandums from all the previous governments and governments of the New Democracy - PASOK and SYRIZA over the last four years. Therefore, its risk and anti-pity are given to the people. By catching the thread from where the SYRIZA government left it, the Government is attempting a series of institutional changes to organise and operate the urban state more effectively in terms of modern management and business administration. Behind the misleading and barbaric slogans of efficiency and colonization, it seeks, first, to form a state administration more centralized, more hostile to the demands of the working people's movement and, at the same time, more effective, more resilient, more resilient to the service of business groups to regain their profitability, more capable of enforcing anti-government policy. Secondly, it seeks to form a core of senior state officials that ensures continuity of this policy, regardless of the political context, any governmental rotation or the intervention of the people. Thirdly, it seeks to regain the people's confidence in executive power, as the evaluation report says, namely to recover its lost credibility from wider popular labour layers in fear of a generalised challenge. That is why the government presents changes in the urban state simply as technocrats, to be lost in the irresponsibleness of the declarations and to blur their class mark. However, it is of little or no interest to the people if the model of the state of service, the civil state, is an Anglo-Saxon or Chancellor type. In any case, it is an anti-linguistic and classic state. The individual disagreements between SYRIZA and the rest of the parties in the bill do not constitute an obstacle to their convergence, provided that a more efficient urban state is guaranteed on behalf of the capital and more aggressive against the people. This is not only the consensus of the SYRIZA and the other parties in the direction and objectives of the draft law, but also the New Democracy's disposition with its Minister to adopt many - if not all - of the proposals it has submitted to the Standing Committee's meetings. After all, on such a major issue, which is the continuation of the urban state and the strengthening of its effectiveness - for which, of course, they are all committed, since they have jointly voted on memoranda and anti-grassroots measures - why should they be ruined by the individual? At such times, the masks fall. The question that arises for us is whether the current state is really ineffective. It is not at all ineffective when it comes to working people's mobilisations by suppression and prosecution, by proclaiming illegal strikes, by the strikes of both SYRIZA and the New Democracy now. It is not at all ineffective when it takes additional tax exemptions and privileges measures to strengthen business groups, when it cuts pensions, breaks wages, legislates labour jungle and expands poverty, when it accelerates electronic auctions, privatises education, health, energy and delivers social security to individuals, when it imposes the law and order on universities and the rest of society. It is not at all weak when he participates in imperialist war plans, when he concludes strategic cooperation plans with the Americans and NATO, in order to facilitate imperialist interventions in the region. The state is doing its job in favour of the social minority, and it seems ineffective and incompetent in terms of the needs and interests of the workers and people's groups. Objectively, what they are left with is the political management of popular anger, how indignation, protest will remain within the framework, be geared to the inability of institutions, persons, mechanisms, the irresponsibility of ministers, will be charged to one or the other political power, but never, ever, but to the class character of the government and the parties in governance, regardless of the management mix that they are impoverished. Ladies and gentlemen, the bill promotes a series of arrangements that form an even more centralised, flexible and tough government, capable of promoting without delay in all areas, all that is needed to create an investment-friendly environment. The Legislative effect report mentions the recovery of investor confidence in institutions, legal certainty and the avoidance of bureaucratic turbulence, so that they can calculate the estimated time to carry out their work and set timelines that can be consistently followed. In other words, it provides for codification in legislation, on the basis of the reactionary European Union law, by updating and permanentising the anti-grassroots framework and the memoranda, a procedure initiated by the SYRIZA Government, in order to achieve a reduction in bureaucracy for investments and business groups. Such a state wants capitalists, a bolted machine that serves the competitiveness and profitability of business groups, the interests of NATO, the Americans and the European Union. This state serves New Democracy, this state also served by SYRIZA in the four years of his administration. Only if one imagines the single and indivisible mechanism of a common mechanical clock, which every small or large gear can only move in a sequence and timing with the rest, will he be able to understand deeper the uniform nature of the multi-faceted mechanism of the urban state and its aims. This objective crosses all the provisions of the bill concerning the organisational restructuring and functioning of the government and governmental bodies, such as the cabinet, government councils and government committees for the organisation of central government and the establishment of the government Presidency as an independent public service. In the first part, the executive role of the Ministerial Council is strengthened, and the role of the Central Council of Economic Policy and the Ministry of Finance is further expanded, so that anti-grass policy can be implemented without impediment. Indeed, the responsibilities of the ERCF are to provide that it will not only be limited to matters of national defence, but, as referred to in Article 5, it will shape the national security strategy, taking into account foreign and defence policy, public policy, cyber security and energy security. It is considered the most appropriate way for an effective holistic governmental approach to the issue of internal and external security, serving the objectives of the European Union and NATO, as set out in the Joint Declaration of the European Union and NATO in Warsaw in 2006, signed by the SYRIZA - ANEL Government and continues today by the New Democracy. In the second part, regarding the principles of good governance of central government, where the principle of efficiency and efficiency must tend to fulfil its mission by using the least resources as far as possible, they are a role of a faster and effective implementation of anti-grassroots policy, on the basis of budgetary commitments and the long term, with fewer resources, which will further reduce social costs in education, health and elsewhere. The establishment of the new office of the presidency of the government next to the Prime Minister, who will coordinate and control the government's work, is an even tighter process in promoting anti-popular policy and the communication strategy for its acceptance by the people. The new role of the General Secretariat for Communication and Information, which is introduced into the draft law and is subject to the Prime Minister, will continue already suffocating and controlling state media for more effective policy propaganda. The objective and pluralist information for the people can only be seen as an alibi. The planning and monitoring of the government project in the third part of the bill, as well as the evaluation thereof, at all times reduces the role of the class urban state to serve the interests of the bourgeoisie and business groups. On the other hand, its role is strengthened, it becomes more difficult to monitor the implementation and the adjustments needed to implement this policy that is friendly to business groups. The unified draft government policy is adapted to the objectives and priorities of the general government's budget, therefore the medium-term financial strategy, namely the government's policy that is a long-standing austerity for popular strata, with bloody primary surpluses by 2060, as decided with the Troika and every time it is legislated by the Government and Parliament. The provision on impediments, incompatibility and rules to avoid conflicts of interest of the members of the Government provided for in Part Four gives the impression of creating a communication image for the integrity of political persons, so that the people will recover their lost in the years of their crisis of confidence in the political system. However, the question of interactiveism does not change, regardless of rules that go in every time to make the game a predetermined rule of legality, since this is mainly about serving the financial interests of business groups. The creation of the National Transparency Authority, in which all control structures will be incorporated to date, will strengthen, as the draft law states, transparency, accountability, integrity and rules of law, against corruption and the opacity that exists today. The moto has heard it too many times before. Behind these well-defined wordings on transparency, the application of the rules of law, in our view, the inspiringness of the civil service is hidden. The essence of the legality which the government is trying to bring about is essentially the legitimacy of the anti-grassroots policies that makes the richer and poorer. It is the legality of unemployment, uninsured work, work to the grave, employers' crimes and imperialist wars. Law is therefore nothing but the will of the sovereign order expressed in laws. This legality is raised by the draft law as a universal need and, if necessary, imposed by law and order, as the evaluation report says. It says "the concept of the rule of law is primarily achieved by safeguarding the prestige of the law". And immediately below it says: 'by improving citizens' compliance with legislation'. It is therefore an element of modernization of this state that is also an attempt to combat corruption and the need to consolidate democracy. In fact, it is also a sign of strong confrontation between the New Democracy, SYRIZA and the Movement of Change, in trying to haunt their superiority towards the other parties on the basis of morality, integrity, competence of their executives and their corresponding lack of these traits in their opponents. This confrontation does not only serve propaganda but also essential purposes. The aim is to facilitate the activity of capitalists in this way. The fact, for example, of the equal access of business groups to tenders or the simplification of procedures for investment are issues which, in fact, are analogous to capitalists. What does the government and the various left-wing defenders of capitalism mean - governments, parties, institutes, personalities - under the term 'corruption'? Do they mean, for example, keeping from all the governments of the law which defines sixty specific tax exemptions enjoyed by shipowners? Do they mean reducing the tax rates for large business groups and, at the same time, the evacuation of taxation and the carving of the people? Do they mean simple state support to the banks when government spending on education, health, social security is being cut? Does it ultimately consider corruption to be the adoption of laws that reduce wages and pensions and increase the taxation of the populace? In no case are these not considered corruption phenomena in the capitalist world. On the other hand, legislative activity for the benefit of the favourable investment environment, i.e. for the benefit of capital, is considered an essential condition for any State which respects its urban nature. "Your fair," said Marx, "is the will of your class that was proclaimed to law." To identify, then, the real content which the parties and the mechanisms of the system give rise to the term 'corruption', we must see the difference between this legislative initiative and those that are characterised as scandals. When it comes to corruption, they mean staff or collective enrichment resulting from breaches of the law. In this category, however, they do not incorporate capitalist enrichment resulting from the faithful application of the law. This infringement usually combines with money, bribery, abuse, extortion of parts of the bourgeoisie. That is why the question of corruption is central to the final state debate. So what does capital need corruption, since it has a whole state mechanism to work for it and turn into a law anything that its profitability demands? Their problem is not the profitability in general, but the greater profitability of each other vis-à-vis the other competitor, the taking up of a project by one company instead of another, the safeguarding of a subsidy or a programme instead of another, the acquisition of larger market shares by one company against competitors. It is clear, therefore, that corruption is the necessary complement to the legitimate pursuit of capitalist profit. Ladies and gentlemen, for the JRC, the labour-class dilemma and popular strata are not 'effective or inefficient state', but whether the state will be the city's ultimate state or the working people's state. The JRC serves the second. This is what we want to build, by the people for the people, a state that does not aim to support the profits of major business groups, but is based on the social ownership of the means of production, labour market control.

It is therefore the government's responsibility, and only whenever it brings changes, to explain specifically and not generally how the best quality of democracy becomes, how its institutions, its country and its citizens benefit from advances. And if it is right and works, let it not just change because it cannot be credited to the present government. But when it changes, we need to know why and how it makes our lives better. So let us see what these new customs are that the new government wants to install and let each citizen draw its conclusions. As far as the Ministerial Council is concerned, your corrective actions are anemic. Although we have pointed it out to you, you have not improved the provisions of Article 2 on off-the-spot matters and on the extraordinary convening of the Ministerial Council. A truly democratic and pluralist approach would like in both cases to take precedence over the majority of the members of the Ministerial Council and not the sole will of the Prime Minister. You also insist on the confidentiality of practices for 30 years, and we ask you what exactly this provision serves. Should the people not be aware of the views of the members of the government at the latest before the next elections take place? Does the full information of voters on persons and things not strengthen the quality of our democracy and transparency? Is that not, in practice, the sense of responsibility of the rulers? What is the point of anyone other than researchers or historians of the future, public access to the minutes after thirty years? With regard to the presidency of the government, following on from the prestigious greed for superpowers and powers, you are setting up a huge centralised bureaucratic structure, which will take on, among other things, the legislative work, without even the guaranteeing presence of the central legislative committee, which is poorly abolished. You thus put your ministers in the upper theory and turn them into simple spectators of developments. You create a new service - umbrella, with annexes to every Ministry, the absolute virtue and the absolute control of Mr. Mitsotakis. You manage it with four hundred forty-three employees, one hundred of whom are posted - here, o of the miracle, they suddenly disappear the affinity blocks - and three hundred seconded officials and journalists, without the sharing of positions. And the members of the Security and Armed Forces, who will assign special tasks to the new structure, are scandalously predicted and paid up up to 30% of their earnings. Fourthly, you are increasing the Secretary-General's general secretariat and creating new ones, which are directly subject to it, while at the same time undermining pluralism by reducing government councils. You have proclaimed small and flexible forms and you end up in a gigantic structure, which is, in many cases, a shadow government and which largely replaces the work of official, at the expense of the principles of democratic legitimacy, transparency and the effective separation of powers. At the same time as you are rushing to entertain the impressions of a state "days" you are giving your general secretaries the opportunity to assign public work to a private person even, as well as, exceptionally, to reward general wage provisions for civil servants who have chosen to participate in committees. At the same time as the supposedly overexcessive civil servants, you avoid making the most of the experienced human resources of the public, preferring it to be privatised. Instead of resorting to the register of directors that the previous government did not take advantage of as it should, you simply abolish it, without any sufficient justification, even though it was valued positively by the OECD. Firstly, while removing the legal form from the ministerial offices, that is, the most demanding and costly task of post-master partners, you are increasing the number of them from the other significantly in relation to the current regime. Finally, the National Transparency Authority. In the well-known pattern, you are creating a new superconcentrating bureaucratic structure, where a number of audit services are merged, which currently employ 12 000 five hundred people. Following the inadmissibility of the penitentiary services to the Ministry of Citizen Protection, according to the unique Orban doctrine, the Ministry of Justice is further weakened by the transfer of the General Secretariat for Combating Corruption to the National Transparency Authority. Remove the Observatory for Public Administration, as you have done with the ILO, you are merging to the new authority as an existing agency the Office of the Public Administration Inspectorate General. You are thus violating the recommendation of the Council of Europe, which requires the functioning of a special, one-off, independent, independent audit body, to supervise the implementation of the National Strategic Action Plan against Corruption and to coordinate the action of individual audits and not just mechanisms. [[Applause from the SYRIZA wing]] Ladies and gentlemen, there is a historical paradox in relation to the Greek maladministration, to which today's bill also comes. This paradox is the fact that we know, at least in the 1952 Varvaressos report, that the administration is the great patient: patronage, pluralism, bureaucracy of many signatures, many and parallel disguised responsibilities, indifference to the daily life of the citizen, has been known since then. How do you explain, then, that three quarters of a century later, despite the efforts made by other governments, especially in the last four and a half years by the SYRIZA government, we have the same problems again? The answer is simple. These pathogens are symptoms. They are manifestations of the needs of a political system that tolerates these features, precisely because it seeks through the consolidation of relations between patronage and anti-corruption, on the top of perpetuating the system of interests that we call a complication, and on the basis of the administrative subsystem to reproduce citizens' dependence on civilian staff with rousphetti, the appointment of others. So when you want to give the contracts to your own people, you don't need a neutral Bemperian public administration. When you want to implement the contract for the Greek, not in the public interest, but in the interests of the private investor, it is reasonable to define the adviser of the State Secretary. The new one perhaps bringing in the new bill is that in this traditional practice of maladministration and patronage of the old political system it is coming to bring a neoliberal shade. The main aim of neoliberalism is to keep the central problems of politics and the economy out of democracy. For example, all operational rules relating to the banking framework, the Basel Agreements, have been laid down by the representatives of central banks, not by government representatives, therefore by representatives of interests and not of peoples. Similarly, you are attempting in the context of this neoliberal viewpoint, which wants to see the administration of the state similar to the administration of a public enterprise, the preservation of the aspects of the administration. Obviously what appears to be preferable to the democratic process, the technological process, confidence in the judgement of the praisers, the experts, simply conceals whose interests are served. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-08-05-S1-commons.u90	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, I really believe that what we are negotiating at the moment is perhaps the most innovative and radical attempt to change the way the state operates. It is a bill that truly deepens, extends, modernises Law 4109/2013, the law that founded the first General Secretariat of Coordination of the Greek State on Government of New Democracy and PASOK and whose structure I had the honour of being the first Secretary-General. What did that law provide for? It provided for the operation of a permanent administrative structure, which would be fully supported by scientific, administrative, technical and government centre, the Prime Minister. We have to say that we have followed the French administrative model by adopting the way in which the General Secretariat of the French Government operates, of course amending its functions so that they can be adapted to Greek data. In addition to the Prime Minister's General Secretariat at that time, which supported his personal political action and the Government's General Secretariat, which followed the legislation from their statement to the House and their resolution, the General Secretariat for the Coordination of Government's Work came to monitor the planning and implementation of all actions between political intention and implementation by regulatory and regulatory acts. What we tried to do then was to create a coherent way of monitoring the government's work. In other words, there were provisions for annual ministries' programmes, annual ministries' plans, on which their political and operational planning was submitted. There were instruments and ways of monitoring quarterly and annual implementation of this programme. There were ways of controlling the effects of all these regulatory and regulatory acts in general. There were ways, in particular, through the Interministerial Committees, even the Secretary-General's capacity to adopt implementing acts for the resolution of disputes between the Ministries, the discovery of synergies and overlapping. All this had been tested in a coherent way and one should say that there was also an innovative and innovative software era, the "DELOS", which was supported by the Prime Minister's General Secretariat for Strategic Planning. Why am I saying all this? I am not saying it just for the history of the matter, I say it for the following reason: when we left January 2015, the Directorate-General for Coordination served about twenty-five people, if I remember well. I learned then that this General Coordinating Secretariat was transferred, that is, fifty more five employees were transferred. What does that mean here? That means, ladies and gentlemen of the SYRIZA, that even though you seem to be reacting to this arrangement, you have honoured the Secretary-General at the time. And not only did you honour her, you kept her in her place, she's still there, Queen Wisdom 15, sixth floor. In fact, not only did you honour it, not just did you use it - and that is to your credit - but you have increased it, you have given it importance, you have given it substance, you have extended it. You have used - and this is also to your honor - your top executives, such as Mr Vernardakis - who will follow and I hope he will say his experiences from this structure - with essential importance to its existence. So why is today's disagreement? In any case, the question is that the bill we are debating today is indeed the completion, extension, deepening, effective implementation of this first law that we have passed, which we have implemented some six years ago. I will not sincerely refer to the letter of the law. Every one of us knows that. I would like to refer to its four only political features. The first political feature of this law is the strengthening of popular sovereignty. The people are voting for politicians, voting for a party, which supports a government for its programme. The unified draft government policy, ladies and gentlemen, is the only essential way for the people to monitor and see whether the government they have voted for, the fact that they have supported plans to do what they have promised. The monitoring bodies for the implementation of government policy are those which give the people - at regular intervals - the opportunity to see whether what they voted for and whether the people who voted actually implement what they have promised. Of course, the subsequent monitoring of the consequences of all regulatory and regulatory acts is what it is doing? It gives the people the final opportunity to decide whether what they voted on is right or not. A second unique political feature of this law: the evaluation of staff. Ladies and gentlemen, the first and only thing that this law does in essence is to enable all - and above all the people - to control civilian staff. The unified draft government policy enables us at the end of the year to see whether the government has succeeded or failed in its work. The action plans of the Ministries enable us at any time to assess the integrity of civil servants, political leaders. At the end of the year, we know whether this Minister and the Secretary-General did these things, which they had promised to do. Ladies and gentlemen, I will not truly forget what the Minister of State said at a meeting of the Council of Ministers, the then Minister for Administrative Reform and Professor of Constitutional Law Mr Antonis Manitakis, from the Democratic Left, referring to such issues. So the professor said that the only way to really assess the staff of any kind, whether politically or administratively, is to have action plans that will have quantified objectives as far as possible. The degree of implementation of these quantified objectives is the only truly reliable way to be able to obtain an assessment of the degree of competence of the staff concerned. What does that mean, ladies and gentlemen? It means that this way of operating and building the state that we are discussing today is possible, whenever we want it, to lead to a real assessment, a truly substantial and reliable assessment of all civil servants at every post in the hierarchy. The level of implementation of the action plans relating to his/her service to the extent that he/she has been assigned can very easily be derived immediately and easily from the degree of integrity of that staff member. Third, a unique political feature: the integrity and emancipation of the public administration. Indeed, in the best European practices, I would remind you of the institution of the German state secretaries, as they are said, who are either servants or parliamentarians and are essentially Undersecretaries, who really have the institution in our case, the institution of the Secretary-General, is the one that actually enables the public administration to stand on its feet, to have the place to develop its skills, to really have the place to operate at the highest technical level and to serve - take care - over time different governments. The pattern is this: Political personnel with the people's mandate sets political objectives and sets out how to achieve these political objectives. It has absolutely nothing to do with either who will implement these objectives or what technical means - and most of all financial instruments - will be used. It is the only way to give the administration the space to step on its feet and actually to work on a maximum level by serving different governments over time. Ladies and gentlemen, the fourth only political feature of this bill is indeed the most hopeful one. I am referring - as strange as it may - to the new provision for the organizational structure of the ministries. In Article 33, paragraph 2, it says that Ministries are now structured in horizontal and support services and in general secretariats. It says, however, that they are also structured in regional services, which have local competence. With two words this means that this way of building the state creates the hope that we will have an end in the central state, but a completely decentralised state, as far as civil service is concerned. Ladies and gentlemen, only when citizens' services leave the central state, they will be transferred to local government and, above all, to primary local government, only when the citizen can serve by the door of his home from one stop shops to anything that concerns him, only then can we be proud that we are living in a European modern state. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-08-05-S1-commons.u121	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, this draft law is a deep part of the country's modern administrative history. The Government of Kyriakos Mitsotakis today brings to the vote not only a law on public administration but the new administrative map of Greece, a manual, as the Minister of State pointed out, which brings together all academic experience in relation to the principles and practices of administration and the appropriate tools of administrative science that will make our state modern and effective. I shall begin with the division of responsibilities between the Minister and the Prime Minister, as well as the establishment of the presidency of the Government. First of all, the administrative order is restored in accordance with our Constitution. The Ministerial Council is strengthened by unloading the Prime Minister, which will more effectively ensure unity, coordination, direction and supervision of government work. A key element in the functioning of the Ministerial Council is that the decisive powers of its members are strengthened on substance, without having to validate post-confidence decisions, while the periodicity of its meetings ensures the deeper interaction of its members, pluralism and democraticity of the Government. In addition, government work will be implemented at rapid pace and targeted actions through collective governmental bodies. I would like to focus here, in particular, on the forecasts for the KYSEA, where this is clearly foreseen, which I have also pointed out for a very long time now. As far as the presidency of the government is concerned, it is now time for the country to acquire a service that supports the Prime Minister and government work on a permanent basis with all the necessary cognitive facilities, ensuring the coordination of cross-sectoral public policies. This is specified here, after promoting the institutional dimension and ensuring continuity of the central public administration, in the light of the public interest. As you can see, ladies and gentlemen, of the four hundred forty-forty employees, the three hundred forty will be civil servants and only one hundred will be posthumous. Let me mention, in relation to the administrative costs of the bill, the prefective structures of the previous government, Mr Polakis, they worked for a hundred fifty poster, while we predict only a hundred. In addition, the general and special secretariats are reduced from ninety-three to fifty-eight. Only for posters the cost savings are calculated by the State General Accounting Office at EUR 1.9 million, while the bill as a whole estimates savings of EUR 2.4 million. At the same time, the distinction between civil and service administrations is served. The Ministries' Secretaries to be appointed through ASP, in conjunction with their coordination services and the strengthening of the responsibilities of the Directors-General, are an extremely important reform that demonstrates the government's confidence in Greek civil servants and ensures the continuity of the administration regardless of party convictions. With regard to the planning of governmental work, with the unified draft government policy, the association of the Ministries' Action Plans, is determined by the Political Monitoring Committee for Government Work and the implementation of public policies through synergies and interactions of the Ministries and their final structures. In addition, this draft law ensures for the Greek public administration the principles of good governance and good administration, as well as the principles of good legislation, i.e. promoting legality, transparency, accountability, efficiency, efficiency, necessity, subsidiarity, merit and professionalism in conjunction with the quality of legislative regulations and democratic legitimacy. Ladies and gentlemen, the quality of the legislative process, as well as the treatment of pluralism and codification of law, are two top objectives for the modern and competitive state. The Presidency of the Government will play a major role in this project through the Secretariat-General for Legal and Parliamentary Affairs and the Central Codification Committee. Furthermore, as the Minister responsible pointed out, among the main objectives of our government is to increase transparency. In combination with internal control units per Ministry, we proceed to the National Transparency Authority, which will absorb the General Secretariat for Combating Corruption, the Public Administration Inspector General, the Public Administration Inspectors' Body, the Health and Welfare Inspectors' Body, the Public Work Inspectors' Body, as well as the Transport Inspectors' College. The new principle will be in line with the international environment and will be the Greek Anti-Fraud Coordination Agency in cooperation with the relevant European authority, which will develop a wide network of controls throughout the public sector. The administration of the Authority will be independent in the performance of its tasks and will be chosen by an independent committee, and will be involved by the Audit Committee and the national audit and accountability coordination body, which will enhance the quality of the audits and, respectively, occupy the entire range of audit bodies in the rest of the public sector. Ladies and gentlemen, our Government is consistent with its election commitments, which we are implementing quickly and even earlier than we have foreseen in the timetable for our programme. Today we are taking a great step forward in order to ensure a strong and competitive homeland in Europe and in the world for our children and the new generation. Kyriakos Mitsotakis and the Government of New Democracy leave a strong footprint on the administrative history of the country and we are still at the beginning. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-08-05-S1-commons.u273	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, there is no doubt that the public administration is the great patient of the Greek state. The New Democracy, its Government, instead of further upgrading and strengthening this reform process, has chosen the way to counter-reformation, to return to a state of partyism, to lack of objectivity and merit, to arbitrary and to promote private interests. The public interest shall be overridden. The current bill is signed: ‘Financial State: Organization, operation and transparency of Government, Government Institutions and Central Public Administration’. His study has shown that this is a false copy. None of the provisions or arrangements contained therein shows the nature of the State as an official and as a State which serves transparency, impartiality, objectivity and merit. It is a state that is responsible for strategically planning, giving general guidelines, coordinating, supervising and controlling administrative executive powers, exercising administrative executive bodies. The implementing powers shall be exercised by the public administration and bodies which perform functions at different levels, from the central administration to the bodies of decentralised administration, regional and local authorities, first degrees. This is decided in the context of the general guidelines, based on specific rules. In this case, such a separation of powers is not established. On the contrary, an over-centralised, pre-centralised system is being introduced, from which all powers are cast. In this context, a new superservice is being created, a bureaucratic monster I would say, the presidency of the government. In this, i.e. the Prime Minister himself, there are six different secretariats, five Secretary-General and one special secretariat, with the division of the General Coordination Secretariat in two secretariats, the General Secretariat of Information, therefore the ERT and the RES - ICM, the creation of a Special Secretariat for Monitoring the Government Work, the establishment of a Legal Board of the State and a Office for the Vice-President of the Court of Auditors and the EEA. The logic of the creation of the presidency of the government is that of a cockpit, I would say, Chancellor of a macho type, of a man's authority, the Prime Minister, who will employ four hundred and forty employees, a hundred of whom will be posted and put. All this, with an increase in existing structures, positions of responsibility, staff positions and a significant burden on the state budget. From the report of the General Accounting Office of the State, only from the establishment and functioning of the Presidency of the Government there is an annual burden on the state budget of at least EUR 227 000. The presidency of the government is not an additional governmental structure, but an over-structure that defines the government structure. The Ministers do not carry out the duties assigned to them by the Constitution and on the basis of which they have the full responsibility of the sector in which they are headed, but are recommended to the Presidency of the Government, which is finally taking a decision. A characteristic example is the exercise of a legislative initiative by the Secretary-General of Legal and Parliamentary Affairs of the Government's Presidency, which, up to now, is the sole responsibility of the ministers under the Constitution and the Rules of Procedure. This is also mentioned in the Report of the Scientific Office of the Parliament. The Prime Minister's relationship with the ministers is absolutely hierarchical, and it should be operational. The Prime Minister should be first among equals, the centre of the network of government functions. This is how the unity of the government is ensured. The opacity and deterioration of the members of the Ministerial Council shall also be reflected in the provision in Article 4(5). In particular, the reference to them, i.e. the official minutes of the Ministerial Council, the debate and the opinions expressed in the adoption of acts or decisions, is prohibited. To date, the minutes contain a short summary of the recommendations, proposals and communications of the members of the Government, opinions and opinions made during the debate on the basis of the a.d. 63/2005. We had laws that are so complex, but at the same time too weak that they can be easily altered by presidential decrees, by joint ministerial decisions, by executive circulars, so that at the end of the day the law can't give it up, but the politician can't give it. Here, you know, the list of consequences for citizens, for society, for the economy is long. I would like, for reasons of time, only to refer to a flagrant case of this category, which, in my opinion, is the conclusion of the construction of a country based on the production of weak, powerless and often void regulatory acts from the point of view of the political class. I shall refer to something that has been heard twice in this Chamber but with a different approach. I refer to the case of the former Public Administration Inspector General, Mr Rakindzis. Note: According to Law 3074/2002, the Public Administration Inspector General is chosen for a five-year term, and is appointed and ceased by presidential decrees. Mr. Rakintzis is in the post of Chief Inspector General of Public Administration on 13-9-2004 and his term of office expires on 13-9-2009. By 2016, however, Mr Rakintzis continues to be a General Inspector for Public Administration. In early 2016, Section III of the Council of State comes and says that the Chief Inspector for Public Administration - precisely because the necessary presidential decrees have not been issued for the continuation of his term of office - is illegally holding this post and the decisions he has made are at risk throughout this period to be considered invalid. Note that from 2009 until 2016, the country was governed by four prime ministers, the governments participated six parties and every year in the House, Mr. Rakinjis came in and gave the report in the account of his actions. Four Prime Ministers, six parties, every year in Parliament, Mr Rakinzis and the President-in-Office of the Council, who would hold him institutionally - regardless of who did not want him, whose friend he was or was not - was not issued, so that his regulatory acts would be void. If that, ladies and gentlemen, is not a clear proof of how brave and great steps we need to take in the institutional armour and modernisation of our legal culture and our operation, then what is it? I submit for the minutes the electronic press articles on this particular incident. [[At this point, the Member, Mr Ioannis Economou, presents for the minutes the above-mentioned publications, which are in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] Ladies and gentlemen, all the things I have described above must be changed if we are finally to build a state. And to change, it is not just the good intentions of people who are in the government or in the parties. That is not enough, because if there is no crystallised institutional shield, good intentions at first will gradually fall below the weight of news and everyday life and then crush themselves at the expense of electoral or establishment approaches. This very pursuit, the crystallised institutional armour, attempts the bill for the final state in four specific directions. First of all, there is a key pillar in the legislative process, which is the Secretariat-General for Legal and Parliamentary Affairs, which will be the only one responsible for the final legislative process of any draft law. At the same time there are some other very serious things going on. Only one I shall refer to. It is well established - and rightly so - that the new laws, in particular those which have a financial and bureaucratic burden on businesses, are valid at certain dates. Secondly, in order to combat corruption more effectively, the National Transparency Authority is created under which all individual audit authorities are gathered and consolidated in terms of public administration and its audit procedures are modernised. Allow me here, Minister, to say that it would be useful for the government to consider to a second degree the corresponding architecture and other control mechanisms, which are primarily necessary, for example, in the fight against blockage, the fight against illegal Greekisation in agricultural products, the chaos of urban transport by means of ticketing and the occupation of buses, which also does not come to an end. Thirdly, the institutionalisation of public administration is guaranteed and the role of civil servants and the new institution of the Secretary-General is substantially strengthened, with its very substantial responsibilities as well as the role of directors-general and with the fact that critical powers of management of control bodies are passed by government or party officials. Wednesday direction: An innovative philosophy of governance is being established with the Ministerial Council to give a single coherent operational governance map and the Prime Minister can actually intervene and resolve coordination issues, which in many cases create arrhythmias and delays. And all this is done with fewer Ministers of State, with fewer general and special secretaries, with fewer posted officials and less costly, as has been pointed out earlier in the statement by the Minister. Ladies and gentlemen, the final state bill, in my opinion, does not just respond to a need for decades. It is recommended for the country and a great opportunity to do things differently, i.e. to finally have a worthwhile manipulated and efficient administration at all levels. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-08-05-S1-commons.u335	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the problems relating to the conditions of governance and the organisation of public administration have been recorded and discussed many times in the past. We have been following with interest the efforts of the Government and the Minister responsible for tabling a comprehensive proposal on the modernisation of public administration and the creation of an effective state at the service of the citizen. The draft law on the final state, which the government has been voting on since the first days of its election, seeks to establish a new system of organisation and hierarchy in public administration. It is true that the way in which the state, its agencies and its services operate is characterised by deep-rooted bureaucracy, dysfunctionality, dyskinesia in many areas, when other European states have solved these issues for many decades. We, as a Change Movement, have tabled concrete proposals for radical combating these phenomena. Ladies and gentlemen, the government comes to solve the chronic problems of public administration with this draft law, which is typically called 'final'. It seeks, through this legislative initiative, that the state now carry out its mission and its work, that it finally achieves transparency and impartiality, fundamental principles of administration. The state at the service of the Greek citizen, with transparency and equality. In this draft law, this is unsuccessful. It is not a state that is decentralised, with the division of powers in local government and in sub-services, but rather a more centralised one with all the decisions to go through the demonstrable presidency of the government, namely, from the prime minister's authority. The Government, under this draft law, has a dangerous increase in the number of posted officials and Advocates-General, but does not allocate equal and rational responsibilities to such persons, but ultimately by concentrating their total on the central government, thus making the State not final and decentralised, but ineffective and over-concentrating. The position of the Change Movement is clear. Principles such as transparency, impartiality, administrative control must be exercised by decentralised agencies. Local government actors must have substantial responsibilities, with social accountability and control by independent authorities, for citizens to intervene and co-formulate through consultation and e-government. So, in fact, this is a centralised state rather than a final one, with the Prime Minister having the final say of every service or issue of the individual services of the public administration, taking away the decisive competence even from the ministers. A balance is therefore sought between political staffing and the necessary autonomy of the administration. On the one hand it maintains its general secretaries and special secretaries, and on the other it establishes the secretaries-general as senior officials. It is trying to respond to the coordination of the Ministries through the transformation of the government's presidency into an executive centre, with a number of staff secretariats. We all know that in the functioning of complex organisations we insist on the need to combine coordination, monitoring and planning with flexibility and initiative. In this sense, the government is a collective process which, in the present draft law, is converted into a pre-central. The performance of the state becomes a pyramid structure, where a central project simply specialises rather than seeks defined cooperation, networking and continuous synergies. Ladies and gentlemen, this is not the state that we want to establish for the moment and the future of Greek citizens, future generations and our children. It is a strict and stiff state that, instead of sharing, accumulates competences in particular persons. In this state the Change Movement states "residents" to confront its program and proposals, but also its parliamentary presence. Thank you very much.

Data from research into an eighty-eight countries indicate that countries with the lowest levels of corruption collect about 4% higher taxes than countries at the same level of economic development, but have higher levels of corruption. I will focus in particular on the chapter of this draft law containing provisions establishing the National Transparency Authority, with key objectives of strengthening the independence and operational efficiency of mechanisms and public accountability policies and combating corruption. In the model of the most effective internationally relevant bodies, the authority will have powers to prevent and investigate phenomena of fraud and corruption, but also to raise awareness of society about transparency and public accountability, for the first time in the history of the country in a single authority the responsibilities necessary for an integrated policy, to strengthen integrity and to combat corruption. The organisational and operational model of the Authority is based on the model of the most successful internationally-successful relevant actors in countries that are in the first positions of the anti-corruption indicators. A key point of criticism of the Vice-Presidents was the provisions of Articles 21 next of the draft law on the establishment of an independent executive agency called the "Principle of Government", which is directly subject to the Prime Minister. These provisions, ladies and gentlemen, form the core of the bill and introduce arrangements to cure chronic diseases. To date, I would remind each Minister that he has taken up a role as coordinator of his Ministry's services. But when we needed an interministerial action, we faced the phenomenon of overlapping powers. The officials were given multiple and sometimes contradictory instructions, thus showing the administration ineffective. This has to stop. With the draft legislation being discussed, the Ministers retain all their responsibilities, while at the same time maintaining full responsibility, as is expressly laid down in Articles 13 and 14. The Prime Minister plans, coordinates and evaluates the government's work at second stage, because he has, according to the law, but also the provision of Article 82(3) of the Constitution, the task of ensuring unity of the government's work and of course bears political and parliamentary responsibility. There is also a common denominator of all the views and observations expressed on the draft law, regardless of their subjective dimension or, possibly, their ideological colours. Furthermore, those attempts to improve structures, as good intentions have so far mobilized them, have found difficulties in their practical implementation. With this draft law, the central administration can now have the final, coordinating and auditing role through the establishment of the government Presidency that will make a decisive contribution to the implementation and assessment of critical public policies. In addition, there is a clear distinction between political and operational administration. This seems to be quite clear in the provision of Article 109 of the draft law, on the basis of which all the responsibilities for the adoption of administrative acts, namely the majority of the administrative material, are transferred to the Directors-General. This reduces bureaucracy and reduces the risks of customer pressure. This demonstrates the government's confidence in the public administration, which offers incentives for efficiency and greater responsibilities. Ladies and gentlemen, I shall briefly refer to the provisions of Articles 57 next of the bill on good legislation. Legislative and legislative control must concern the agreement of the law with over-legislative provisions and the Constitution, as well as its integration into the body of existing legislation. The legislative process aims at fewer and better laws, which in Greece is constantly demanded. The final state introduces a General Secretariat as the only body responsible for the final legislative process of any bill proposed by the Minister. Another independent multidisciplinary committee is established to evaluate the quality of the draft law and its consequences analysis. Although it did not result from the Government's draft law to discontinue or intervene in any way in the procedures under way, it was clarified by the Minister and provided in the bill that no existing or ongoing controls would be interrupted by the creation of the principle of transparency. On the other hand, all the checks carried out are being carried out regularly, as are the orders in question. Ladies and gentlemen, every piece of legislation is, of course, the question of the application of the provisions contained in it. Let us leave the triumphant designations of opposition and stand by the substance of the bill. Finally, Greece is the division that has been taking place since decades in all advanced administrative action systems between political and service administrations. The first has a regulatory and strategic role, the second has an operational role. This prevents and inactivates corruption and reduces bureaucracy. It seeks the efficiency of the state. Planning and evaluation of the implementation of decisions are introduced. So let me be allowed to paraphrase. This draft law is not only summarised in the final state but in the words ‘end state’. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-08-05-S1-commons.u347	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, with the final state bill there is a leading administrative reform. A new integrated and modern plan for the organisation, operation and transparency of the government, government institutions and central government is being put in place with a uniform and coherent regulation of all individual issues and the abolition of all fragmentary arrangements that are dispersed into dozens of laws and regulations. But why is this bill so important? I will not tire you, repeating the content of the provisions, which I am sure we have all studied in detail. I will only say a few words about the substance and the importance of the new arrangements. Upgrading the Ministerial Council overall changes the way the Government operates, which will now operate with programme, unity and cohesion. The Ministerial Council will, from now on, collectively approve the annual planning of government policy and, in addition, monitor and collectively evaluate its implementation in order to coordinate governmental work and at last produce concrete results. The creation of the presidency of the government is necessary for the Prime Minister of the country to have the infrastructure and human resources which will enable him to respond to the institutional role which the Constitution reserves to him. This is achieved by the organization of the relevant services and the division of responsibilities in the Secretariats of the Presidency of the Government. The introduction of adhesives, incompatible and rules to avoid conflicts of interest ensures the impartial exercise of government or administrative tasks. It is precisely for this reason that these impediments concern not only the period of performance of the tasks concerned, but also the period after leaving the post. The ban on recruitment as poster employees of spouses or relatives of the government puts an end to the family rule. The seasons that one Minister or State Secretary hired members of his family or, in order not to be exposed himself, hired another Minister, go unreturned. Finally, the recruitment of relatives to the government. The introduction of the new framework for good legislation puts an end to the uncontrolled production of laws. By simplifying and codifying existing legislation, pluralism and evil are dealt with. All the pathogens of legislation that lead to lawlessness are treated. The subjection of government administrative action to principles and rules contributes significantly to the effective protection of citizens' rights. I am thinking in particular of the principles of good governance, good administration, transparency, accountability, efficiency, efficiency, necessity, subsidiarity, merit and professionalism. These authorities are now specific content with corresponding administrative obligations. The reduction in the Secretary-General from ninety-three to fifty-eight does not only imply the better, faster and more effective functioning of the public administration. It also generates savings of EUR 1.8 million per year in the state budget. The establishment of a permanent Secretary to each Ministry ends the party state. The delegation to the Directors-General of the competent final signature for each individual administrative act of their services, together with the corresponding provision for the Secretaries-General, entails a drastic reduction in bureaucracy and corruption. The establishment of the National Transparency Authority is expected to make a significant contribution to the integration, coordination and upgrading of the control mechanisms that are part of it, to enhance transparency and to combat corruption more effectively. The draft law on the final state lays the foundations for a modern efficient State that operates in practice to serve the public interest. It may seem too ambitious for the data of Greek reality, as we used to it to date. But to change the situation, someone always has to start over. The elections of 7 July closed a period of social, economic, political and cultural regression. Our goal is to self-powered Greece, a Greece that runs hard and goes forward, with unity, schedule and hard work. Our Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis and the government worked for a "new normality" that means progress and not return to old pathogens. Thank you very much.
